**The kind is signed.** `Create` carries `options` as the count of answers and `Kind.Categorical`; `Enter` carries the answer's index as `value` and 10000 as `confidenceBps`; a vote names an answer's index or the void. `valueAllowed` and `outcomeAllowed` take the count of answers; the browser names an answer as `a:<index>` (`callToOutcome`), beside `n:<number>` for a number question. Nothing goes into `dare_number_series` for a pick-one market: a pick is a choice, not a number, so there is no aggregate, no marker and no leader (3.31).

## 2026-09-26: The screens, per the doc

Built to 3.29, 3.30, 3.31 and 3.25 with the sheet rows of 3.24. **Asking**: the third chip under the band, "Pick one", opening the answers editor: one 44px row per answer on the surface with the 28px slot, the answer as an inline field and a remove control; a dashed "Add an answer" row while there are fewer than six; "Add a person" with the people as 32px avatars in 44px targets, one tap adding that person as an answer (the asker last, as "You"), the ones already added at 0.35 opacity; the one caption. "Next: who's in" stays disabled under two. **Entering**: the sheet rests on "Pick one" and the answers as 44px radio rows (`PickOneEntry`, a control for the type budget); a tap takes the market's field, a 1.5px inset ring in your hue and a check, and raises the stake chips and the primary, "I'm in: John, $10". The entry line reads "You're in: John" and Change reopens the rows with "Save: Priya, $10" beside "Never mind". The primary reads "Pick an answer", disabled, until a tap: 3.30 has the primary appear with the pick, but the pinned sheet never goes empty (3.24) and the other two kinds keep a disabled primary before a touch, so the same is done here; recorded as a reading. **Where the stake sits** (`PickOneBars`): one row per answer with a 12px bar whose fill is the answer's share of everything riding, your stake in your hue at the left end of your pick's bar behind a 2px gap in the ground, your 22px avatar on that row, shares by largest remainder from the third entry (`answerShares`), an answer nobody picked as an empty track and 0%, no marker and no leader, the caption only when one stake is more than half (4.9, `pickOneCaption`), and the entering moment (bars from the left over 700ms, your share, your avatar, the shares last). **Blind**: outlined tracks, your pick's 5px cap in your hue and your avatar, the lock chip and the count, and no bars sent before lock, since where everyone landed is the shape. **Voting**: the answers as equal wells two across with their avatars, "3 of 6 say Priya. Two more and it settles.", "That's right, Priya", and "Not how I saw it" raising the sheet to "What did you see?" with the other answers as rows, a dashed "I couldn't tell" (the contract's void) and "Never mind"; a split names the answer nearest to settling ("Two more for Priya and it settles."). The modal's chalk is "Say it: Priya". **Settled**: the outcome names the answer with the claimant's words when they are short ("Priya, twenty minutes in."), the caption says who called it in the design's four shapes (`calledItLine`: "Theo called it. Nobody else did.", "Theo and Maya called it.", "Four of you called it: Theo, Maya, Gabe and John.", "Nobody called it."), and "Everyone's pick" (`PickOneRows`) replaces the call line and closest first: a 48px row per answer with its pickers' 24px avatars overlapping by 6px, the called row washed in the market's ink at 0.40 with a 6px cream cap and its pickers in the extra cream ring, then who's got who, or "Nothing changes hands." when nobody or everybody called it. Who called it, on a pick-one question, is everyone who picked the answer that happened, never the first to say so. The same rows stand on the story card (3.4), on the memory view where the call line would, and on a void with nothing washed. **Everywhere the viewer is an answer, they read as "You"** (the sheet's rows per 3.30, and the bars, the rows and the count line for the same reason the rest of the app says You); a person answer reads by their current first name to everyone else. The entry line names the answer and nothing else (4.6); the count line says "say" rather than "have said" because the doc's line does.

**The tiles** (3.27): the asking tile's frame line is "Pick one." and its empty answer is the answers themselves, up to six rows in a 420px column centred in the safe square, a 44px avatar where an answer is a person; the result tile carries the answer at 72px ("Priya."), the same rows with the pickers' 44px avatars and the called answer washed, and "Theo called it." Neither shows a share, and the photos line works the same. **The proposal and the tiebreaker** have answer variants (`proposeAnswer`, `arbitrateAnswer`), which name an answer by its number in the asker's list or say the terms do not decide it, with the same rule for screenshots; an answer past the list reads as a void rather than a number. Recorded fixtures: `scope-pick-one`, `propose-answer`, `arbitrate-answer`. **A notice** about a pick-one market says "Decided." and opens the screen, as a number market's does: the answer is the asker's to write and the group's to read, and a notice goes out on its own to everyone; rejected: naming the answer, which would be the first notice to carry what a question came out at.

## 2026-09-26: You counts pick one and never plots it

`calibrationFor` already left categorical positions out of the yes-or-no bins and the number record; it now counts them (`pickOne: { resolved, called }`, `pickOneCalls`: a full score is the pick that happened) for the line 3.34 gives them, "You called 5 of the 9 you were in." The You tab is still undrawn, so this is data.

## 2026-09-26: Gas, re-measured for the first pick-one market

The survey's `pickone` mode (five answers, every pick at 10000, resolving with half right and half wrong, the most edges a pick-one market can mint): `create` 608,257 for two positions and 938,602 for five, 388k plus 110k a position; `resolve` 261,550 for one edge and 853,615 for six, 143k plus 118k an edge; VOID 74k. Within six percent of the yes-or-no and number figures on `create`, and inside the per-edge room the limit already carries, so the limits in `gas.ts` stand for all three kinds and the figures are recorded there.

## 2026-09-26: Not built, and asked

**A blind pick-one market with no close time** (3.38: "locks when everyone asked is in, or when the asker locks it: its band reads 'Locks when all 6 are in'") is not built, and asked about rather than read. No kind has a market without a close time today: every dare signs `resolvesBy`, the tick expires a void-rule question the moment it is past due and the tiebreaker may be asked the moment an arbitrate-rule one is, so a signed deadline of zero would end the question at lock. It needs its own rule for the scheduler and the details before it is a question of screens. **Multi-choice spreading** (3.36) waits for the contract redeploy, as the doc marks it. **Adding a photo while a market is still open** waits for the design session deciding where it goes. **What's on** (3.32, 3.33, 3.35) is the separate phase after this one; pick one is complete enough for it to use: a public template is a question, terms, two to six answers and a close time, and everything from entry to the tile takes them.

# The open-window photo, and the ninth design session (2026-09-26)

## 2026-09-26: The ninth design session's files, in their places

The design session edited its own copy from the session before, not the repository's, so the two were compared before anything was replaced: `docs/design.md` had not changed since the photos round (the categorical phase touched `docs/decisions.md`, `docs/testing.md` and `CLAUDE.md`, never the design), and every hunk of the diff fell inside what the session listed: the new 3.39 and section 8, the short edits to 1.1, 1.5, 1.8, 3.8, 3.24, 3.34, 4.7, 4.8, 6.3 and 7, and the intro's board list with "What changed in this revision" rewritten for the ninth session alone. Nothing to carry over. The new files replaced the two under their canonical names and the dropped copies are gone; the export draws the new boards (`OpenPhoto`, `LightScreens`, `LightRules`). What in this revision touches code: the photo while the market is open (below); 1.5's rule that nothing is frosted or translucent in either theme, which the build meets already (the tab bar, the pinned sheet and every modal sheet are opaque surfaces with a line, and the only translucency is a scrim on a photo, with no blur, so nothing changes); 4.7 now points at 3.15 for the one citron dot per viewport, which is how "Needs you" already draws it; and 3.8's line that whoever added a memory can remove it (built below). The light theme (section 8) and the "Appearance" row on You (3.34) are marked for after submission and are not built. Rejected: taking the new file whole without the comparison, which would have lost any amendment the categorical round had made; there was none, and the comparison is what says so.

## 2026-09-26: A photo while the market is open

Built to 3.39 as written. The rule the doc gives and the door did not have: a memory taken before the market ends is its author's alone until then, then everyone's the market's rule lets see it. It is one pure function (`memoryVisible`, `src/lib/media/roles.ts`) asked in two places: at the door (`canSee`, which `/api/media/[id]` answers 404 from, so the other participant fetching the id gets nothing, not even that it exists) and in the reader every screen goes through (`mediaOnMarket`, whose frame and memories are empty before the end and whose new `yours` list is the viewer's own). The test refuses the other participant and a group member at the door before the end, finds the photo in nobody's frame and not in what the model reads (`evidenceFor` reads evidence only, and a memory is never evidence), then ends the market by hand and finds it in the frame for both. Rejected: enforcing it on the screen alone, which is what the brief warned against, and a flag on the row, since whether a memory is visible is a fact about the market's state and its author, both already on the row.

**Who, and when.** `memoryAllowed` now admits a memory from the moment a market is asked, for someone who is in it, in every state but a draft (a draft has nobody in it). Two readings recorded: a photo started before lock lands as a memory even if it finishes after (3.39, "Edges"), so the rule admits a memory on a locked market too, and the button itself goes at lock; and the "Yours from tonight" row is shown to its author on the locked and voting screens as well as the open one, since 3.39 says nobody *else* sees it there, and hiding a person's own photo from them until the end would leave them nothing to remove it from. Rejected: refusing a memory at lock, which would lose a photo taken a second before; and the row on the open screen only.

**Where.** The camera is a 56px square secondary button (`CameraButton`) at the end of the "Send it to the chat" row in the "you're in" sheet, which `InviteShare` now takes as a trailing control; it renders only where that sheet does (someone who is in, while the market is open) and only where photos are on, and it opens the one shared picker (`PhotoAdding`) in its camera mode (`capture="environment"`, one photo at a time) rather than the library. Someone not yet in sees the entering sheet and no camera, no input and no row: entering stays the open screen's only move. The row (`OpenPhotos`) sits under the participant stack with the label, the 60px squares (four then "+N"), and the caption; a photo going up is its square at 0.88 with the 2px runner, a failed one keeps its square with the camera glyph and a tap retries it, and a landed one keeps its preview until the screen has re-read and shows the stored copy, so a square never blinks out between the two (found in the real session: the first cut dropped the preview before the server's copy arrived). Rejected: a second picker for the camera, when the sheet's chalk, the empty slot and the camera are one act with one input.

**Full screen, Save, Remove.** A tap on a square, or on the frame's photo once the market has ended, opens `PhotoView`: the frame derivative on the ground, a 48px close, "Save to your phone" and, for whoever added a memory, "Remove". Saving fetches the bytes through the app's own door (which signs a one-minute URL after the check; the bucket answers a browser's fetch, checked in the development browser) and hands the file to the share sheet where there is one, so an iPhone offers "Save Image" for a photo the camera in a web app never put in its photos; where there is no share sheet the browser saves the file (the development pane took that path). Removing is destructive (3.12), so it asks once in the modal sheet, then `removeMarketPhoto` deletes the row before the objects; only the author, only a memory (`removeAllowed`); evidence stays, since a vote or a ruling may rest on it, and the test tries to remove a screenshot by its own supplier and is refused. The frame's photos open the same view, so 3.8's "remove it from its full-screen view at any time" holds after the end as well as before; the frame had no full-screen view until now, and this is the smallest one that meets the sentence. Rejected: a download link, which iOS ignores for a cross-origin image and which never reaches the photo library; and removal from the strip, which 3.8 places in the full-screen view.

**Not touched.** The proposal and the tiebreaker read `evidenceFor`, which reads evidence rows only, so a memory never reaches a model whatever its timing; the tile never carries a photo; adding sends nobody anything. Owed on production: the camera from a phone, "Save to your phone" through a real share sheet, and the other participant refused at the door from a second real session (docs/testing.md, session 14).

# Public markets: What's on underneath its screens (2026-09-26)

The owner's five answers and the go-ahead came with two decisions that change the What's on screens (one row per game, and a game page listing the questions a group is running), which a design session is drawing. This round builds everything underneath them and holds the screens: the feed and its adapter, the second source, the polling, the templates and their terms, the two new kinds of market, the backstop with its three endings, and the two notices. Every decision below names its rejected alternative.

## 2026-09-26: The middle outcome, checked first

The owner's who-wins question is the chance the home team wins, drawn as a slider between the two teams, with a tie scored as the exact middle. The deployed contract was asked first, the way the pick-one confidence was: `scoreBinary` takes 0 or 1 and reverts `BadOutcome` on anything else, and `resolve` and `arbitrate` refuse a binary outcome above 1 (`_requireValidOutcome`), so a yes-or-no market cannot resolve to 5000 on this contract; the check is a permanent test beside the other contract cross-checks (`tests/db/scoring-chain.test.ts`), and the mirror refuses the same. As ruled: until the redeploy an NFL tie goes unsettled with no toll against the asker, and the middle outcome is on the redeploy list beside multi-choice. Built: the who-wins template is an ordinary binary market storing the home side's chance, with the odds line's ends named for the two teams (the away side low, the home side high) and its outcome words the sides' ("The Giants won", "The Titans won"); a final that is level proposes nothing to vote on (a quorum's void would count against the asker) and the final score voids it a day on as `feed`, with the ruling saying it was a tie. MLB and NBA cannot tie and NHL resolves in overtime and the shootout, so only football's terms carry the tie line. Rejected: scoring a tie as no, which the owner ruled out; and a pick-one of the two teams, which would need a third answer for a tie.

## 2026-09-26: The data source, behind an adapter

The schedule and the results come from a public scoreboard, the same JSON its own site loads, read behind one adapter (`src/lib/sports/espn.ts`) so a licensed provider replaces it by changing one file; the brief's reasoning stands in the log as given (unofficial and unlicensed is acceptable for a prototype reading a public scoreboard; licensing matters if public markets ever become a revenue path through a league). Nothing in the app says "official". The fields are read the way the recorded responses showed them (`tests/fixtures/sports`, recorded by `scripts/dev/record-sports.ts`, the way the model's responses are): the status's own `completed` is the only word that a game is over, and `winner` is never read (a game in progress shows both sides false, and a completed game where neither is the winner is a tie); the score is a string, parsed before anything compares it; `playByPlayAvailable` gates the first-drive question, and `timeValid` gates listing a game with a close time. The recording caught three baseball games in progress and a rain delay, and each reads as no result whatever the scores say. Anything missing, malformed or unparseable is no game or no result: a broken event is left out of a day and the rest read, an answer that is not a scoreboard (no `events`) is a feed error and never a day with no games, and nothing here can settle a market wrong. **The second source** (`balldontlie.ts`) was checked with the real key before anything rested on it: the free tier returns games with final scores for NFL, MLB and NBA (the recorded finals, with runs from the innings' data for baseball) and refuses hockey with a 401, so a hockey game never has a second source. It allows five requests a minute a sport and has been reported slow: one call reads a day, the answer is kept on the game row, and it is asked only from the tick, once per game, never while a person waits. A game is matched by both abbreviations through a short alias table where the two sources spell a team differently (Washington, the Warriors, the Pelicans, the Knicks, the Spurs, Utah, Jacksonville, the Rams, the Athletics); an unmatched game reads as no result, which is the safe side. Rejected: matching on names, which the two sources spell more differently than abbreviations; and a per-team lookup, five calls a minute being the ceiling.

**Polling** is the tick's. One sport's schedule is refreshed at a time, the stalest first, every thirty minutes, five days ahead, one listing a day (`syncSchedule`, `sportDueForSync`), and "Try again" on the failed-feed state re-reads it on demand (an assumption confirmed by the owner). After a game's expected end (the start plus the sport's usual length: 3h15 football, 3h baseball, 2h30 basketball, 2h45 hockey) the games that a question rides on are read every ten minutes until complete, then once more half an hour on to catch a correction (`CONFIRM_AFTER_MS`), and then not again. A final that changes between reads restarts its clock and moves every proposal that depends on it; a final that stands is confirmed. The proposal (3.35) is written on every locked, undecided question the score answers (`dares.feed_outcome`, in the chain's encoding) and follows the feed until the quorum is reached; once a market resolves on the chain it is final. The market's pulse includes the proposal's time, so the ballot re-reads when the score lands. Rejected: polling every listed game, when only a game with a question on it needs a final; and polling until the day's end, when a confirmed final is enough.

## 2026-09-26: The templates, their terms and their scales

Four questions a game can carry (`src/lib/sports/templates.ts`), in the curators' order: who wins, the margin, the total, and the first drive only where the scoreboard reports play-by-play, which in the recording it does for no upcoming game and every finished one; so the first-drive question will be offered nowhere until the source reports play-by-play ahead of a game, which is reported here rather than worked around. No quarters or halves. Everything closes when the game starts (`resolves_by` is the start, so the tick's lock fires at kickoff), and the terms say the consent in plain words, as the owner asked: "If nobody votes, the final score decides." Every What's on market's tiebreaker is fixed to the final score (`stalemate = 'arbitrate'`, with the row written and not tappable on the terms step, and `arbitrateMarket` refusing the model on a question the score decides); the first-drive question is the one the score cannot answer, and it keeps the ordinary tiebreaker. The rows people set themselves stay theirs: who's in, what's riding, and whether it is blind. The wording never says spread, odds, bet or official, and the test says so. A public question's market wears the sport's mark from the emoji table (football and basketball Clay, hockey Ochre, baseball hashing), the assumption the owner confirmed.

**The margin** is a signed number market centred on zero, the home side's score minus the away side's, and never called a spread anywhere a person can read it. The chain stores nothing below zero, so every value is shifted up by half the scale before storing and back down for display (`toStored`, `fromStored`), which changes no score: a miss is a difference, and a difference is unmoved by a shift. The unit carries the shift and the two sides (`Unit.margin`), so every place a number is said (`unitPhrase`, the entry line, the wells, the count line, the ruler's ends, the settled answer) reads "Giants by 7", "Titans by 3" or "Level", and the axis's column labels read as signed figures. The field takes an away-side margin as far as half the scale (`MarginEntry` clamps there), and a final past that floors, which the outcome says (`floored`); the alternative, a shift of the whole scale, was rejected because the owner named half and the floor only bites past a miss the scale already scores at nothing. The scales, per sport, chosen here and never derived from entries: totals 28 points (football, typical 45), 10 runs (typical 9), 40 points (basketball, typical 225), 6 goals (typical 6); margins 28, 8, 26 and 6, each about two typical margins so half of it covers most games. A template's scale is stored as the template's (`range_source = 'template'`, migration `0024_whats_on`) and shown in the details like an asker's, since people wrote it; storing it as the asker's would make the data say something untrue. The far-off check reads a template's scale as it reads an asker's.

**Underneath the held screens**, the two new kinds use what exists: the odds line with the team names at its ends (`OddsLine.ends`) for who wins, and the number entry behind a side chooser (`MarginEntry`: the away side, Level, the home side, then by how many) for the margin, which is enough to test them. The ask flow from a public question (3.33) is built as `/m/new?template=<id>`: it starts at who's in with the template's question under "From What's on · Titans at Giants", the terms step is the written rows in ink-2 with the stake and the reveal as usual and "Send it" as the chalk, and the draft copies everything from the template on the server (`draftFromTemplate`, `draftFromTemplateAction`), refusing a game that has started or whose time is not confirmed. There is no tab and no game page: the operator script `scripts/dev/whats-on.ts` reads a sport's schedule and lists the questions with their ids until the design lands. The market carries one extra details row, "Question from | What's on, Titans at Giants", and "Decided | By the final score, once the game is over"; nothing on the band, the tiles or the notices says where it came from. The use count exists as data (`useCounts`: sets with a market from the template once a second person is in), for the screens to come.

## 2026-09-26: The backstop, with an ending on every path

The brief's rule left a market waiting for the group forever when the sources disagreed or there was no second source; the owner replaced it. Built as ruled, as one pure decision (`backstopDecision`) the tick applies to at most two markets a minute: both sources reporting the same final settles it a day after the final was first seen; the scoreboard alone, with the second source having nothing (hockey always, or the check down or late), settles it three days after, provided its final stood its confirmation re-read; two different finals void it, with no toll, a real conflict meaning something is wrong and a guess being worse than nothing changing hands. Before its time it waits, and a conflict before the day is up waits too, since a correction may still align them. The second source is asked once per game when the day is up, kept on the row, and asked again at the three-day mark for a game it had nothing on. **The ending** is recorded on the chain through `arbitrate`, as an arbitration is, with the ruling's hash and the ruling text "Decided by the final score, as the terms said. Giants 24, Titans 17." (a tie and a conflict carry their own sentences), and mirrored as `resolved_by = 'feed'`: counted clean in the asker's rate, and a feed void in neither number, since a tie the contract cannot score and two scoreboards disagreeing are nobody's fault. It follows from the tiebreaker being the final score, which everyone agreed to at entry. The tick's own arbitration backstop skips a question the score decides, and the "Can't agree?" ask is not offered on one. **On the ballot** (3.35): before the score is in, the sheet reads "The final score will propose what happened.", and "Say it yourself" appears two hours past the game's expected end in case the feed is late or has nothing; once the score is in and nobody has said, the source card stands where the claim card would (the ticket kicker, the two rows with the losing side in ink-2, "Final, Sun 4:12pm. The terms said the final score decides.") and the sheet's header names the score, with the chalk confirming it in the market's own words; once someone has voted, the claim card is theirs as usual. The settled screen credits the final score, not the tiebreaker, and says nobody called it in time. Rejected: settling a conflict on the scoreboard; waiting forever; and a new ending outside the contract, which would have left the chain without the resolution.

## 2026-09-26: One warning before a backstop acts, one notice after

For What's on markets and for the existing backstop on ordinary ones, whether that is the tiebreaker or the void rule: one notice before the backstop acts and one after it has, by push and email only (texting is not set up), and never a second reminder, the person-triggered nudge covering everything else. The warning goes six hours before the earliest ending (`BACKSTOP_WARNING_MS`): eighteen hours after a final was seen on a question the score decides, eighteen hours after an ordinary question was due or locked under the tiebreaker, and six hours before the void rule's deadline where a window remains (a void-rule question the tick locks at its deadline expires the same minute and gets no warning, since there is nothing anyone could do). Once per market (`dares.backstop_warned_at`, claimed before sending), to everyone in it, with the flavour in its words. The notice after goes when the final score settled it, when the final score could not, and when the void rule let it expire; the tiebreaker's own ruling notice already existed. The wording is provisional and flagged for the design session's pass (`backstopWarningNotice`, `backstopResultNotice`), and neither carries a number or a score. **Recorded tension**: these are caused by time passing, which Principle 1 forbids; the owner decided them, and `notification_log.caused_by` names the asker, as the deadline notice does, the rule everyone agreed to at entry being the asker's act. Rejected: a second reminder; a notice to the group it was asked in beyond the people in it.

## 2026-09-26: Held, asked about, and not built

**Held for the design**: the What's on tab (one row per game), the game page listing the questions a group is running, each opening its ordinary market screen, the asker choosing which questions to start with and anyone adding another, the first-run starters from What's on (3.14), and the tab bar's fourth item. **Reported rather than built**: the first-drive question, gated on play-by-play the source reports for no upcoming game. **Not built**: settling anything from play-by-play; the middle outcome and multi-choice, which wait for the redeploy; the light theme.


# What's on and the game page (2026-09-26)

The tenth design session drew What's on as games, the game page, the two sliders between two teams, the endings, the notices and the ballot's wording, and the build under them was already in. This round builds those screens to the doc and aligns four things the build had done before the design existed. Every decision below names its rejected alternative.

## 2026-09-26: The tenth design session's files, in their places

`docs/design.md` had not been amended during public markets (no commit touched it since the ninth swap, and the working tree was clean), so the tenth session's file replaced it whole, with the export beside it. Two amendments made here, both the owner's: the tie scored at the middle (3.40, frame 4) stays drawn and is marked for the contract redeploy in the text and on its board, since the redeploy is exactly when it becomes buildable, and the void version is the one built; and the curated event row with no teams (3.32, "Tuesday's Wild Card games") is marked for after submission in the text and on its board, since every What's on row the build lists comes from the feed and nothing stands behind a curated event. Rejected: deleting the middle version, which the session had suggested.

## 2026-09-26: Where the build changed to match the design

**The warning goes by push, else email, never both** (4.10), through one pure rule (`pushElseEmail`) the two backstop notices send by; everything else still goes on every channel that takes it, since a channel that drops it is the reason "Needs you" exists. **A warning that would land at night moves to 8pm the evening before** (`warningSendTime`: between 11pm and 8am in the person's zone). The zone is the asker's (`dares.zone`, kept from the display cookie at draft), which stands in for everyone in the question, since a friend group that sees each other weekly shares one; rejected: a per-user zone column, because nothing collects one today (a push subscription could carry it later, and an email-only person would still have none). The tick now works out the exact moment each backstop acts (`backstopMoment`: the tiebreaker a day after due or locked, the void rule at its deadline, the final score a day after the final when a second result exists and three days when none does, the play-by-play three days on) and sends six hours before it, moved by the overnight rule, once, and not at all when the votes already cast would decide the market or the moment has already passed. To say which ending is coming, the second source is asked once at warning time and kept on the row for the backstop. **The notice after replaces the ordinary result notice** (4.10): the tick's tiebreaker ruling and the void rule's expiry now send the backstop notice, not the ruling notice; a ruling someone pressed for still sends the ruling notice, since a person acted. The wording is 4.10's table, the question as the title and one sentence as the body, the email's subject the sentence with the one line under it. **The line at entry is "If nobody votes, the final score settles it."**, in the entry sheet directly above the primary with the ticket glyph (3.35, 4.9), in the templates' terms, and in the game's terms step; the details' "If it's unclear" row carries the whole rule ("The final score. If the two results we check disagree, it's void."). **A game's story adds each pair's consequences together** across its questions (3.4), display only: one summed row per unit and pair, and the obligations underneath stay separate, one per question, each the viewer is owed keeping its own settle row under the sum; a pair who owe each other within a game cancel out through the existing netting row on the person view. Rejected: a combined obligation, which the brief ruled out. **The curated event row is not built.**

## 2026-09-26: The first drive, by coverage, from the play-by-play

The brief's gate was wrong and is replaced. `playByPlayAvailable` reports whether play-by-play exists yet, which before kickoff it never does (the recorded slate shows it false on every upcoming game and true on every finished one), so a question closing at kickoff could never be offered on it. The first drive is now offered by coverage: football in the feed's regular season (`season.type` 2, read off the scoreboard and kept on the game row), where the league's play-by-play is reliably published; not the preseason, not another sport. Rejected: offering it everywhere football is listed, since the preseason's coverage is not reliable. It is resolved from the same source's summary once the game has it (`espn-nfl-summary-final`, the Packers game, recorded by `scripts/dev/record-sports.ts`): the first of the drives the source lists, when it began in the first period, its own word mapped to the five answers by an explicit table (`driveAnswer`: TD, FG, PUNT, INT, INT TD, FUMBLE, FUMBLE TD, DOWNS, MISSED FG, SAFETY, END OF HALF, END OF GAME, every one seen in a recorded summary or named by the terms). A word the table does not know is no result and falls to "Say it yourself"; it is never guessed into "Something else". The tick reads the summary from half an hour after kickoff, every ten minutes until the drive stood a re-read, for games a first-drive question rides on, and not again once the game is complete and read since. One source only, since the second source's free key has no play-by-play, so it settles on the 72-hour rule: three days after the drive was first read, once a later read found it unchanged. **The ending nobody had ruled on**: a drive the play-by-play could not say (no drives, or an unknown word) three days after the game was complete voids, recorded as `feed` with the ending `drive_unknown`, against nobody. The owner had ruled that every backstop path has an ending and that a real conflict is worse than a guess; this follows it. Rejected: waiting forever, and the model's tiebreaker, which would count a void against the asker for a question they did not write.

**The ballot on the first drive** opens on a source card "From the play-by-play" once the drive is read, with the answer as its one row and no avatar and no "says", and the chalk confirms it in a tap; this departs from 3.35, which said a first-drive question never gets a source card, because the brief's section 4 has the play-by-play propose it. Reported here for the design session rather than worked around.

## 2026-09-26: The tab, the game page and its start

**Routes**: `/on` is the tab, `/on/[id]` the game page (`?g=` picks which set of people, `?add=` opens the terms step for one more question), and `/on/[id]/[g]` the link sent to the chat, which carries its tile (3.27). The set of people is in the link's path and never inferred, so a preview bot fetching a game's link with no session can only draw the questions of the set the link names, never another group's. Rejected: the tile reading the most recent group on the game, which would show one group's asker and questions to anyone holding the game id (3.32: nothing about any group the person is not in). **Starting a game** makes one ordinary market per chosen question in the menu's order (`startGame`), all with the same people, all closing at kickoff, one Stakes row for them all, and then signs the asker's `Create` for each on the client (the ledger wallet, silently) so the group sees the questions at once; the asker then enters from each card like anyone. Rejected: entering at "Send it", which would put four odds lines on one screen. A `Create` that fails to sign leaves that question a draft the asker's page lists as theirs to finish. Blind reveal is not offered on a game's terms step, because 3.38's step lists the Stakes card alone; "Add another" opens the same terms step for one question with the same people. **The game page has no pinned sheet while the game is ahead** (3.33), so "Send it to the chat" for the game page's link is a plain row under the cards; "More" holds each question's terms. **The cards' "Voting ends" clock** is a day after the final was seen, which is the ending the warning names when both results agree; the alone path is later and the card does not pretend to know it. **Whose page**: the sets of people the viewer is in with anything started on the game, most recent first, with the context chips (3.19) under the header when there are two or more.

**On Now and in a timeline** (4.7, 3.4): a game with more than one question in the same set is one row (`collapseGames`, in the section of its most pressing question, with that question's verb opening the question and the row opening the game page) and one story (`GameCard`, the final score as its subject, the frame with every question's photos, one line per question with what the two in view said); a game with one question is that question's ordinary row and story. **The night** (3.37): the game page once the game is over, the final score as the title, every question's photos in one frame (the claims' attachments first, then the memories in the order added), the settled cards, who's got who summed across the night, "The rest of that night" leaving out the game's own questions, and a sheet with the photo move alone, which attaches to the game's first question. **Team stamps** (1.7): the feed's abbreviation on the feed's colour, six hex digits kept on the game row, in whichever of graphite and cream has more contrast by relative luminance, with the inset ring, a quarter radius, and no logo anywhere; the colour appears in the stamp and nowhere else (4.5), so the fill, the wash and the columns keep the market's ink. **The two sliders** (3.40) are one component (`TeamLine`): the stamps growing and shrinking by 18 + 42v, the fill from the middle, the margin past an end typed, "A tie" at zero and "Tie" on the axis; the chip entry from the previous round is retired. **"No spread at all."** is gone from the weight line's caption, now "Everyone on one number." (3.22, 4.6).


# Delegation (2026-09-27)

The riskiest build left: the server signing routine ledger actions with a share Dynamic hands it. One property stands above everything: the server can write the ledger but never cast a vote, and the governance wallet is never delegated. The owner set a gate before any of it is built, four checks that prove the governance wallet is out of reach, and two smaller things first. This section records the two smaller things, the gate's first result and what the other three wait on, the instrument built to run them, and every decision with its rejected alternative.

## 2026-09-27: The chain suite's one failure was a sibling process's cleanup, not the relayer

What actually failed. The run at 23:21:50 UTC on September 26 ran the settle suite and the sports suite in one `node --test` call, which runs test files in separate processes side by side. The sports suite's third test (the backstop on the chain) failed thirty seconds in, before it reached the chain, and the error text was lost to the grep that filtered the output. Recovered from two places: the feed table (`sports_feed_reads`) holds the NFL schedule read's last error at 23:22:18, "Failed query: insert into public_questions", written by the test's own schedule sync (production's NFL sync runs at :13 and :43, and its source is named the same), and the Postgres log at 23:22:19 says why: `insert or update on table "public_questions" violates foreign key constraint "public_questions_game_id_sports_games_id_fk"`. The game row the test had just written was deleted between its two inserts. The settle suite had finished, and the fixture's cleanup removed every game whose source id starts with `test:`, which was every run's, not its own. The two earlier failures in the same session ("Pick a time that hasn't passed.") were a test drafting from a game that had started, fixed in the session. Not an RPC timeout, not a nonce collision, not an ordering race on the relayer.

Fixed at the cause: a suite registers its own prefix (`track.gamePrefix("test:<run>:")`) and cleanup removes games under the registered prefixes and no others (`removeTestGames`), with a test that plants another run's game beside its own and expects it untouched; the sweep (`npm run test:sweep`), which runs alone and by hand, still takes every `test:` game. Rejected: running the suites one at a time (the rule is that a test cleans up its rows, and a cleanup that widens past them is the bug whatever the schedule), and tracking game ids one by one (the sync makes rows the test never sees).

## 2026-09-27: The relayer's nonce, one lock for every sender

Found by inspection while looking for the failure above, and not its cause: the relayer read the pending nonce from the RPC on every send and had nothing that kept two senders apart. Every serverless instance, the tick, the development machine and the tests send with one key, so two of them in the same second would send with the same nonce and the second would fail loudly for a person, which is exactly the kind of failure the owner named as one that surfaces while a judge is watching (four people confirming after a resolution tap within seconds of each other). Built: the nonce is read and the transaction sent under one transaction-scoped advisory lock in the shared database, the one thing every sender shares (`withSendLock`, `pg_advisory_xact_lock`, released with the transaction if a process dies), the receipt is waited for outside it, and a send that the node still answers with a nonce collision (a lagging node behind a load balancer that has not seen the last send) is resent with the next nonce, three attempts, while any other failure is thrown at once (`sendWithNonceRetry`, `isNonceProblem`). The simulate stays before the lock. Tested: the retry's rule on a fake sender, and the lock against the real database with two senders that must not overlap. Rejected: viem's in-process nonce manager alone, which keeps one process's sends apart and does nothing for two instances; a stored last-used nonce, which adds a row the RPC already holds and goes wrong the first time a transaction is dropped.

## 2026-09-27: The consent line on a game with the first drive on it

The line at entry, "If nobody votes, the final score settles it.", is the consent the backstop rests on, and on a game page with the first drive among the questions it was untrue for that question; the play-by-play settles it. The terms step already showed a combined sentence when the chosen questions' consents differed, and the combination read badly ("and the play-by-play the first drive"). Now one rule (`consentFor`): the score's line for questions the score settles, the play-by-play's for the first drive alone, and with both chosen one sentence that is true for every question on the page: "If nobody votes, the final score settles the others and the play-by-play settles the first drive." Each question's own market screen keeps its own line, as before. Rejected: a consent line under each question's terms rows, which 3.33 does not draw (the step has one consent line, above the button) and which would repeat a sentence down a list. Recorded as a dated amendment in `docs/design.md` 3.33.

## 2026-09-27: The delegation gate: the first check passes from the code and Dynamic's own records; the other three wait on the console

**Check 1, the code.** The app makes both wallets with two separate calls to Dynamic's `createWalletAccount` (`wallet-bootstrap.tsx`, one at a time), each a key generation of its own, and the server assigns the first as the ledger wallet and the second as the governance wallet. Dynamic's read-only API, asked about the two test accounts, confirms it below the code: each wallet is its own credential with its own share set (`shareSetId`) and its own key share, and each key share carries a different `keygenId`, the id of the ceremony that generated it. The two wallets share a derivation path (`m/44'/60'/0'/0/0`, the first account of a root), which is the path from two different roots, not two paths from one: had they been derived from one root they would share a `keygenId` and differ in path. Both are `TWO_OF_TWO`, the person's share set is `rootUser`, and neither has another share set yet. The values are recorded in docs/testing.md session 17 as the baseline for check 3. Passed.

**Checks 2, 3 and 4** need delegated access enabled in Dynamic's console with an HTTPS endpoint and a public key, which the owner does; nothing else can (the API token is read-only, and the console is the owner's). The instrument to run them the moment that is done is built and tested here: the webhook door, the store, the signer with its refusal, a development-only page that triggers delegation for the ledger wallet by address, and a script that runs the three checks and prints the result (`scripts/dev/delegation-gate.ts`). Check 3's "no reshare of its own" is read from the same API: after the ledger wallet is delegated, its `otherShareSets` gains a delegated share set and the governance wallet's `keygenId`, share set and key share must be exactly the baseline. Check 4 tries the export the Java SDK documents through the Node SDK's nearest equivalent (the server wallet client authenticated with the per-wallet key); whatever comes back is discarded, and no code path in the app calls export.

**The limit, honestly.** A passing gate proves that Dynamic's API refuses, and that Dynamic's records describe two key generations; it does not prove the key material is mathematically separate. That structural claim rests on the per-wallet reshare and per-wallet keys in the documentation. Sound for a testnet hackathon; on the mainnet list to confirm properly with Dynamic.

## 2026-09-27: What the instrument stores, and how

**The webhook** (`/api/delegation`, `receiveDelegationEvent`): the body is read as bytes and the `x-dynamic-signature-256` header is checked as HMAC-SHA256 over exactly those bytes, in constant time and whole, never over a re-serialised object (the docs' example re-serialises; the conventions forbid it, and Dynamic's own webhook page says raw body). The `ping` Dynamic sends when an endpoint is registered arrives before its secret can be anywhere and carries nothing, so it alone is answered without a signature. Events are idempotent by `eventId` and ordered by Dynamic's `timestamp`: a replay is a no-op and an older event than the row's is stale, so a late replay of a grant can never undo a revocation. An event about a Dynamic user we have no row for answers 422, so Dynamic keeps it to replay from the console, and material that will not decrypt answers 400 rather than storing a blank. Nothing logs the body, and the decrypt's own error is never returned, since Dynamic's message could quote what it failed to decrypt.

**The refusal, three times.** A `created` event for the governance wallet, or for an address that is neither of the person's two, is refused by address (`delegationRefusal`) before anything is decrypted, and the test proves the decrypt is never reached; the database's trigger (migration 0001) refuses the row again; and the signer refuses the governance address before it looks anything up (`GovernanceNeverDelegated`, a bug in the caller and never a fallback), so no future code path can reach a delegated governance wallet by accident.

**The store.** The share and the per-wallet key are decrypted with Dynamic's own function and the private key held only in the environment (`DYNAMIC_DELEGATION_PRIVATE_KEY`, the base64 of the PEM or the PEM), then sealed again under the app's own key (`DELEGATION_STORE_KEY`, AES-256-GCM, a fresh IV each time) and bound to their row (the user and wallet ids as associated data), so one row's material can never be read as another's. The plaintext buffers are zeroed after sealing. Dynamic's `shareSetId`, when the event carries one, is kept and passed back at signing. **Revocation wipes the material** (both columns become empty) and sets `revoked_at`, keeping the row as the record that a delegation existed and when it ended; the brief said delete what is stored, PLANNING.md said set `revoked_at`, and this does both. Rejected: deleting the row, which loses the trace. A later grant overwrites the same row with fresh material and clears the revocation.

**The signer** (`delegatedWalletFor`, `signWithDelegation`): the ledger wallet with a usable row opens its material, signs EIP-712 typed data through Dynamic's delegated client (`delegatedSignTypedData`, which the package exports although its reference page omits it), checks the signature recovers to the ledger wallet before returning it (a wrong signer is thrown, never sent), and records each signature with the request that caused it in `delegated_signatures` (the action, the subject, the digest, the server action or route, and the platform's request id). No row, a revoked row, or material that will not open is `DelegationUnavailable`, which means the caller prompts as today.

**The environment.** Four server-only variables, blank in `.env.example`: `DYNAMIC_WEBHOOK_SECRET`, `DYNAMIC_DELEGATION_PRIVATE_KEY` (an RSA key of at least 3072 bits: Dynamic's console refuses anything smaller, which the owner found at setup), `DELEGATION_STORE_KEY` (the same value locally and in Vercel, or one cannot read what the other stored, as the VAPID pair is) and `DYNAMIC_SERVER_API_TOKEN`, a second token with the two delegation scopes only (`waas.authenticate`, `waas.delegatedAccess.signMessage`), so the read-only token stays read-only. Two packages added, `@dynamic-labs-wallet/node-evm` and `@dynamic-labs-wallet/node`, loaded only where they are used.

**The trigger for the gate** is a development-only page (`/dev/delegation`, `notFound` outside development) with Dynamic's `delegateKeyShares` for the ledger wallet by address and `revokeDelegation`; the webhook it causes lands on the deployed endpoint whichever origin triggered it, and the shared database carries the result back. It is not the consent moment and carries none of its words.

## 2026-09-27: The consent moment is not designed, and is asked about

`docs/design.md` says nothing about when delegation is asked for or how someone turns it off, and the brief says to ask rather than invent, since it is a moment of consent whose wording matters. Asked, not built. What is known for the design session: Dynamic's own approval sheet (`initDelegationProcess`) says "Wallet delegation approval", "{{appName}} is requesting access to perform automations on your behalf. You can revoke this permission at any time." and "I understand, and agree to accept this permission request", with a learn-more that says wallet, trading bots and an em dash; every one of those strings is overridable through the SDK's `locale` prop (`dyn_wallet_delegation.*`), and the sheet can be skipped altogether, since `delegateKeyShares` delegates a wallet with no Dynamic UI, which leaves the app's own framing to carry the explanation. Whether the reshare itself shows anything of Dynamic's is learned in the sandbox at check 2. The console's "prompt users on sign in" stays off, because the app asks at its own moment.

## 2026-09-27: Membership is not widened by delegation

Delegation hands the server a share that signs ledger actions: confirming, closing, netting, creating a market, entering. It signs nothing about membership, which stays relayer-only through submission exactly as before, and it produces no governance signature, so it cannot vote or, once membership changes are governance-signed, change a quorum. The residual risk already on the record (2026-09-17: a compromised relayer registering fabricated members and, with delegated ledger shares, entering positions for a real user) is the reason governance-signed membership is on the mainnet list, and this phase neither widens it nor closes it.

## 2026-09-27: Before mainnet, added

Three items join the mainnet list (2026-09-17, the membership entry; PLANNING.md 15.1 and 15.8): Dynamic's Enterprise plan, which production delegated access requires; a proper confirmation from Dynamic that a user's two wallets are separate keys, beyond what the gate can show; and the export behaviour, whatever check 4 finds, since a server that can export the ledger key holds the key outright rather than half of a signing pair.


## 2026-09-27: Two more things before the gate ran, at the owner's asking

**The locked send has a timeout.** A node that never answers would have held the relayer's send lock for as long as the transport's retries allowed, and every send behind it with it. The locked part (the nonce read and one broadcast, seconds when the node answers) is now given up after fifteen seconds (`SEND_TIMEOUT_MS`, `withTimeout`, `SendTimedOut`), releasing the lock; a transaction that was broadcast before the stall is counted by the next sender's pending-nonce read or caught by its retry, and the action that timed out fails loudly to its person as any failed send does. Rejected: shortening the transport's own timeout, which every read shares and which a slow receipt wait legitimately needs.

**Every delegated signer failure is a prompt.** The app's actions get one entry point, `trySignWithDelegation`, which returns a signature or null and never throws: no delegation, a revoked one, material that will not open, Dynamic's package failing to load (its native binary missing on the platform, which a deploy's tracing may not carry), Dynamic refusing or unreachable, a signature that recovers to the wrong key, and even a caller asking for the governance wallet (logged as the bug it is). Null means the client prompts exactly as it does today, so nothing about delegation can fail the person's action. The honest path is tested end to end with the test's own ledger key standing in for Dynamic's signing, and records the signature with its request. Rejected: letting the routine failures throw and having each action catch them, which is the pattern that turns one forgotten catch into a broken confirm.

## 2026-09-27: The gate's second check, first run: the real delivery is not the documentation's example

With delegated access enabled in the console (the owner found Dynamic refuses an RSA key under 3072 bits), the door deployed, and the SDK's cached project settings in the development tab refreshed by hand (it had "delegated access: off" from before the console change; the memory already recorded that cache), delegation was triggered from `/dev/delegation` for the localhost test account. The SDK's `delegateKeyShares` for the ledger wallet by address showed no Dynamic sheet at all and reported the ledger wallet delegated and the governance wallet still pending. Dynamic delivered `wallet.delegation.created` to production three times; production answered 400 each time, and the sandbox's three attempts were spent. Dynamic's own delivery record (read through the API's webhook messages, payloads never printed) showed why: the real event carries `userId` as null at the top level and the user's id inside `data`, where the documentation's example had it at the top, and the door's schema required it there. Two corrections: the id is read from `data` first, and the real delivery's shape is kept as a recorded fixture with its ciphertexts blanked (`tests/fixtures/dynamic/delegation-created.json`), which the unit test parses and the database test's events now mirror. The record also showed the door had checked the event's shape before the signature, so the 400 said nothing about whether the signature verified; the order is now the ping, then the signature, then the shape, so an unsigned body learns nothing about what the door expects. **The SDK matches a wallet by its own address casing**: the control now hands it its own entry for the ledger wallet, found by lowercase equality, since the users row keeps addresses lowercase and the SDK holds them checksummed.

**A replay was tried and refused itself.** `scripts/dev/delegation-replay.ts` takes a delivery Dynamic recorded, checks its signature exactly as the door does, and only then pushes it through the door's code here. The record keeps the body parsed, not as the bytes sent, and no re-serialisation (the record's order, sorted keys, the documentation's order, compact or spaced) reproduced the signature, so the replay did not run. Rejected: replaying without the signature, which would have stored the material and let checks 3 and 4 run today, and proved nothing about the door. Check 2 waits on the fix being deployed and a fresh delegation (revoke, then delegate again from the same page), which is also when check 3's comparison of the governance wallet against its baseline, and check 4, can run. Also seen in the webhook's event list: `wallet.delegation.signature`, an event per delegated signature, which the door ignores today and which could later stand as Dynamic's own audit trail beside `delegated_signatures`.

## 2026-09-27: The gate's second check, second run: the delivery was read and the private key was not

With the corrected door deployed (commit 2c801a2, confirmed by an unsigned malformed body answering 401 and a signed one 400), delegation was turned off and on again from the development page in the localhost session: Dynamic's revocation reshare and its fresh delegation both ran with no sheet of Dynamic's on screen, the revocation's event was delivered and ignored (200, no row to wipe), and the fresh created event reached the door's private-key step, where production answered 500 three times. A diagnostic probe of the deployed door (a created-shaped event I signed with the webhook secret for the localhost test account's ledger address with junk envelopes, which reaches the private-key check and nothing further) answered "no delegation private key configured": the value in Vercel is not readable as a PEM or its base64 there, while the same value is here (a 3072-bit key; the console refuses smaller). The reader now also takes the forms a console field leaves a value in: surrounding quotes, line-wrapped base64, and the PEM with the two characters backslash-n for its newlines; a PEM cut short is nothing. Check 2 waits on the variable being set in Vercel's production environment and a redeploy, then one more off and on. Meanwhile Dynamic's record already answers check 3's comparison: after delegation the ledger wallet carries a delegated share set (`otherShareSets: [{shareSetType: "delegated", TWO_OF_TWO}]`) and the governance wallet is exactly its baseline (share set 962198bc, keygen FBFJFrnk, no other share sets).

**Whether the governance wallet can be marked ineligible at Dynamic's end.** It can be marked denied, per wallet: the SDK's `denyWalletDelegation(walletId)` calls Dynamic's wallet-settings endpoint with `hasDeniedDelegatedAccess: true`, Dynamic stores it on the wallet, and the SDK's auto-prompt and `shouldPromptWalletDelegation` then skip that wallet. It is a preference the user's client sets, not a rule Dynamic enforces: an explicit delegate call for a denied wallet is still allowed by the SDK (its eligibility filter admits denied wallets), and the read-only environment API cannot set it. So it narrows the auto-prompt and nothing else. Recorded as a rule: **"Prompt users on sign in" stays off permanently**, since that prompt offers every undelegated embedded wallet, the governance wallet included, and the app asks at its own moment for the ledger wallet by address. Marking the governance wallet denied at wallet creation is a cheap second belt for the build phase (the prompt is off anyway), noted and not built before the gate. And **the door acknowledges, discards and alerts**: a created event for a governance wallet is answered 200 so Dynamic stops, nothing is decrypted or stored, and the owner gets one ops email naming the user, the wallet and the event, never the material; tested with the alert injected, and its mutant (an alert to nobody) killed.

## 2026-09-27: A send is never lost: the timeout is not a failure, a pending send is said to be on its way, and the tick finishes it

The fifteen-second timeout on the locked send, as built the round before, would have failed the person's action for a transaction that may well have been broadcast. Rebuilt so that a send is never lost. **The relayer signs locally** (`prepareTransactionRequest`, then the account's `signTransaction`), so the hash is known before anything is broadcast, and records the write in `chain_writes` (the hash, the label, the kind of mirror it needs and the ids for it, the nonce, the signed bytes) before the broadcast; the lock now covers the nonce read, the signing and the broadcast alone. **A broadcast the node stopped answering releases the lock and carries on**: the same signed bytes are broadcast again outside the lock ("already known" counts as accepted) and the receipt is waited for as before, so the person waits a little longer and sees the result, not a failure. **Only when no receipt comes within twenty seconds** (`RECEIPT_TIMEOUT_MS`; Monad mines in under a second, so this is the node, not the chain) does the action end with `SendPending`, which the ledger modules pass up untouched and the actions turn into one line, "Sent, and still going through. Nothing more to do here; it will show in a minute." (`SEND_PENDING_COPY`), in the slot a refusal would take; the pending state as a mark rather than a sentence is for the design session, since nothing draws it. **The tick finishes it** (`reconcileChainWrites`, once a minute, at most ten writes): a pending write older than a minute has its receipt read; mined, the kind's completion writes the mirror the action would have (`src/lib/ledger/completions.ts`: the confirm's shadow rows from the proposals alone, the close's clock, the lock's mirror, a settlement from the indexer with its ruling and ending, an expiry, a registration's id), and a completion the indexer cannot yet serve is asked again next minute; no receipt, the signed bytes are broadcast again, five times over half an hour, after which the write is dropped; a nonce that went to something else drops it at once; and a drop, or a revert after the person was told it was on its way, sends the owner one ops email, since the person's screen will not show what they were told to expect. A request that saw its receipt marks its own write complete, so the tick never writes a mirror twice; every completion is idempotent anyway. Every write the relayer has made since is on the record: seventeen from the test suites on the real chain, all mined and completed in their own request. Rejected: keeping the send inside the request until the receipt regardless of time (a serverless function has a ceiling and the person a patience); a pending state without a completion (the person told to expect something the app would never show); and dropping the record of a write once mined (the trace is the point).

**What a test run leaves behind.** `chain_writes` rows for the transactions a suite made on the real chain stay, mined and completed: they are the relayer's record of real transactions, like the chain's own history, and not the test's rows. The reconciler's own test scopes to its hashes and removes them.

## 2026-09-27: The gate's third run: all four checks pass, with the limit stated

With `DYNAMIC_DELEGATION_PRIVATE_KEY` readable in Vercel's production environment, delegation was turned off and on again from the development page in the real localhost session. **Check 2:** Dynamic's revocation event was delivered and ignored (no material to wipe, since the previous grant had never been stored), and the fresh `wallet.delegation.created` event was answered 200 on the first delivery and stored: one row for the ledger wallet, carrying Dynamic's delegated share set id, the event id and the sealed material, and no row for the governance wallet. **Check 3:** this module refuses the governance address before any lookup; the ledger wallet's delegation signs a check message that recovers to the ledger wallet, recorded in `delegated_signatures` with the script as its request; a signing request for the governance wallet's id made with the ledger wallet's materials is refused by Dynamic ("Authorization header or cookie is required": the per-wallet key authorizes nothing for another wallet, so the request is unauthenticated rather than merely wrong); and Dynamic's record after delegation shows the ledger wallet with one delegated share set and the governance wallet exactly at its baseline from session 17 (share set 962198bc, key share 314140da, keygen FBFJFrnk, no other share sets). **Check 4:** the Node SDK's nearest export path, the server-wallet client authenticated with the per-wallet key and then `exportPrivateKey` with the delegated share, is refused with 401 at authentication, and the delegated client exposes no export at all; nothing came back and nothing was stored. No code path in the app calls export.

**The limit, as before.** A passing gate proves that Dynamic's API refuses and that Dynamic's records describe two key generations, one per wallet; it does not prove the key material is mathematically separate. That confirmation is on the mainnet list. One stray line at the script's exit, the delegated client logging a WebSocket reset, is its socket being torn down by the exiting process after the check signature had already recovered correctly; noted, and not a failure of any check.

## 2026-09-27: A told send that never landed is the person's to do again

The round before told a person whose send outlived its request that it was on its way, and told the owner by email if the tick then dropped it or found it reverted; the person's screen said nothing. Now `chain_writes` records whose tap a write was (`actor_id`, the person acting in the request, null for the scheduler) and when they were told (`told_at`, set only when the request ends pending, since a request that saw the receipt or a plain failure already said what happened), and Now reads back the person's told sends that were dropped or reverted (`src/lib/ledger/again.ts`). A dropped confirm is said on the proposal's own row, which is still there because nothing was recorded ("Didn't go through last time" as the reason line, the one place words may stand beside a mark on a needs-you row, and the same line on the yep screen beside the button); a dropped lock on the asker's lock row, or on a row of its own when there is none; a settlement, a forgiveness or a cancelling out on a row of its own ("Settling with Gabe", "Calling it even with Gabe", "Cancelling out with Gabe", the open ring in the person's hue, "Try again"), which opens the person view where the move is. A row leaves when a later send for the same thing exists (whoever made it, whatever became of it, unless it too was dropped), when the thing is no longer open (the proposal answered, the obligation closed, the question locked or decided), or a week on. A pending registration (a group or a unit reaching the ledger for the first time, on someone's first confirm) is setup rather than the person's own action: its line now says so ("Still setting things up. Nothing was recorded; try again in a minute.", `SETUP_PENDING_COPY`), the tick lands the registration, and the person's next tap goes through; it is never a row, because they were never told it would show. Rejected: a notification (nobody acted, and Principle 1 forbids a notice about a state of the world); a row that stays until dismissed (a control for a rare state, and a week-old row says nothing true); and showing a drop the person's own request already reported as a failure. The database suites' fixture and the sweep unhook a temporary person from the relayer's record before removing them, since the record stays.

## 2026-09-27: The tick finishes a resolution the votes already decided

A resolution is the one write a person cannot do again: the last voter's tap submitted `resolve` with the signatures already stored, and if that send was dropped after they were told it was on its way, or their request failed past the vote, the question stayed undecided until someone changed a vote. The tick now finds a locked, undecided question whose stored votes reach its threshold, checks that no send for it is in flight or mined and waiting on its mirror (`writeInFlight`, by the write's kind and subject), asks the indexer once, and resolves it from the signatures there (`resolveFromVotes`, the same path the last vote takes), with the result notice sent as that last vote's, since the vote is what decided it. A send the tick makes whose receipt outlives the tick is the reconciler's to finish and not a failed job. Rejected: a row asking the last voter to vote again (nothing of theirs is missing), and leaving it to `castVote` alone.

## 2026-09-27: The governance wallet is marked denied at creation

Built, as the second belt behind "Prompt users on sign in" staying off: a new account's governance wallet is marked denied for delegation at Dynamic's end in the same moment the account is made (`/api/session` says when it created the row; `wallet-bootstrap.tsx` then calls the SDK's `denyWalletDelegation` with the credential id read from the login token, the same token the server counts wallets from, since the SDK's own lists fill in slowly after a login). It is a preference Dynamic stores on the wallet (`hasDeniedDelegatedAccess`) that its sign-in prompt honours, not a rule it enforces; the refusals in the door, the database and the signer stay the enforcement, and a failure here costs nobody their account. The development page marks an existing account's wallet the same way, which is how it was verified: in the real localhost session the SDK's status for the governance wallet went to denied and Dynamic's record shows `hasDeniedDelegatedAccess: true` on the governance wallet, `false` on the ledger wallet, with the ledger wallet's delegation untouched. The creation path itself needs a fresh account, which needs a code the owner types; it is on the phone list. Rejected: marking it from the server (the read-only API cannot; the SDK call is the user's own client setting a preference on their own wallet).

## 2026-09-27: What delegation would remove, flow by flow

Asked for before anything past the instrument is decided: every flow that writes the ledger, what a person confirms today, what they would confirm with delegation, and which wallet signs. Dynamic's own signing sheet is off (2026-09-20), so on a device that holds the person's Dynamic login no flow shows a signing confirmation today: the app's own button is the whole act, and the SDK signs behind it with no UI. What each flow signs, and how often:

| Flow | Wallet | Signatures behind the tap | Confirmations today | With delegation |
| --- | --- | --- | --- | --- |
| Enter a question (yes or no, a number, a pick) | ledger, `Enter` | 1 | 1 (the app's button) | 1 |
| Ask a question and enter it | ledger, `Create` then `Enter` | 2 | 1 | 1 |
| Start a game with N questions | ledger, `Create` per question | N, one after another with the question's title shown while it signs | 1 ("Send it") | 1 |
| Confirm a cover (yep) | ledger, `Confirm` | 1 | 1 | 1 |
| Confirm a batch at claim time | ledger, `ConfirmMany` | 1 over the batch | 1 | 1 |
| Settle or forgive | ledger, `Close` | 1 | 1 (the sheet's button) | 1 |
| Cancel out both ways | ledger, `Net` | 1 | 1 | 1 |
| Vote on an outcome | governance, `Vote` | 1 | the app's own short sheet, then its button | unchanged by rule |
| Lock, resolve, arbitrate, expire, the feed's settle, register | relayer alone | 0 | 0 | 0 |
| The settlement photo, a memory, a nudge | none | 0 | 0 | 0 |

So delegation removes nothing visible on the device where the person signed in. What it changes is elsewhere: on a device that holds the Dareful session and not the Dynamic login (a second phone, the installed app after the browser's storage was cleared), every ledger action today detours through the code step first (`useSigner` opens it and carries on after), and with delegation the server would sign from the session alone; a confirm from a notification without opening the app, which only a server-held share could do, and which iOS cannot offer (next entry); and host mode, where a friend enters on the host's phone under their own delegation, which works because entering is signed by the ledger wallet. The owner may stop at the gate on this table; nothing past the instrument is built.

## 2026-09-27: Notifications on iOS carry no actions

Whether the installed app on iOS could confirm from a notification: no. Web Push on iOS and iPadOS (16.4 and later, for a web app added to the Home Screen) delivers a title, a body, a tag and data, and shows the app's icon; action buttons are not part of that surface, and `Notification.maxActions` is 0 where an engine has no action support. Chrome on Android does render actions. So a confirm from a notification is not available to this product on the phones the owner tests on, and it is not a reason to build delegation. Read from WebKit's announcement of Web Push for web apps on iOS (webkit.org/blog/13878), a 2026 survey of PWA limits on iOS (magicbell.com/blog/pwa-ios-limitations-safari-support-complete-guide), the browser table for notification actions (web-push-notifications.com), and Apple's developer forum thread 726793 on actions in 16.4.

## 2026-09-27: Entering without an account was never wired; now it is the front door

**Which it was.** Never wired, not a regression. The market link page has answered a visitor with no session with the sign-in gate since binary markets (2A), and nothing ever wrote a position for a ghost: `dare_positions.claim_id` and its acknowledgement column, the two limits in the conventions (one position per browser token per market, ten ghosts per link) and the bind that rewrites positions have been in the schema and the claims module since Phase 1, waiting; no revision of `src/lib/ledger/markets.ts` or of the page ever reached them.

**Built, to docs/design.md 3.17 and PLANNING.md section 4.** A visitor with no session gets the market's own screen in its ink (`src/app/m/[id]/ghost.tsx`): the wordmark where back would be, the question band with the state mark and the clock, "Priya asked the Friday crew", the participant stack with a count in words and nothing else, two facts and the tiebreaker, and the sheet at rest with the line untouched; no stakes, no amounts, no leaderboard, no obligations, no names beyond the asker's and the avatars'. Sliding raises the sheet, which asks who they are, and "Join at 70%, $10" puts the number in with no account, no wallet and no signature (`enterAsGhost`): the position is a ghost's (`participant_claims`, made by the asker's link, carrying the hash of the number they gave if they gave one), acknowledged at once, the ghost a member of the set (a named set; a pair's dyad takes nobody new), and the browser keeps a token for the ghost, issued on that tap, so the same browser is the same ghost next time: the number is theirs to change until lock, the same number from another browser is the asker's same ghost, and nothing is doubled. The ghost binds to whoever signs in from that browser or with that number, through the two paths every ghost already has (2026-09-18), and once bound the position is that account's, unsigned; on the market screen keeping it or changing it is the `Enter` signature that makes the question ordinary ("This was you before you signed in. Keep it, or change it."). Every kind: a yes-or-no question, a number (checked as anyone's is), a pick, and a What's on question with its two teams; a game link opened with no account lists the set's questions on the game, each opening its own screen.

**Rulings the doc did not cover, or that differ from it, for the design session.** (1) "We text a code" is not said: nothing is texted at entry (Dynamic's code is a sign-in, which makes an account, which Principle 7 and the owner's flow both rule out at this moment), and the caption says what is true; the phone number is the binding key and is optional. (2) A name is asked for, or one of the set's ghosts is picked ("Is one of these you?", PLANNING.md step 4), since everyone else's screen needs a name for the position; 3.17 draws no name field. (3) The stake chips show at entry, since a position has a stake; 3.17 defers them to after the code. (4) A typed name is acknowledged at once, not held for the asker's tap (PLANNING.md step 5): a question's set is whoever joins (2026-09-19), a judge or a friend arriving by link cannot wait on the asker, and the two limits hold. (5) The signed-out page names the asker by first name and the set, as 3.17 draws, which supersedes the 2026-09-18 rule that a signed-out page says no more than the card for market links; a cover link is unchanged, and the http suite's check was brought to the design. (6) A number the entrant types is hashed at the edge and kept on the ghost only, never resolved to an account at entry: the answer to the request is the same whether or not the number has an account (the oracle, 2026-09-18), and binding does the rest at that account's next login. (7) `entered_by` is the asker for a ghost's own entry: the column is not null and the asker's link is what let them in. (8) A ghost's joining is not yet announced: the joined notice names a person with an account. Rejected: Dynamic's code in the sheet (an account at entry), and the sign-in gate staying (the first minute for a judge or an invited friend, which the track's criteria weigh most).

## 2026-09-27: A provisional market: locked here, decided by its account-holders, settled to proposals

Nothing goes onchain for a position nobody signed (PLANNING.md section 4), so a question with a ghost in it, or with a position bound to an account and never signed, is provisional from lock (`src/lib/ledger/provisional.ts`): `lockMarket` records the moment and closes the room without a chain write, with the threshold the contract's rule over the account-holders who can vote (the account-holders in it and the asker, who alone records the outcome when nobody else has an account); the ballot is the same, the votes the same governance signatures, verified against that quorum (`quorumOf` answers it for a provisional market, so the notices and the nudge follow); and at the threshold the market settles here, by the contract's own scoring rule mirrored in `src/lib/ledger/scoring.ts`, to one pending obligation proposal per transfer, from the lower scorer to the higher, with the ghost on whichever side the ghost is, `origin = 'dare'`, and scores and nets on the positions. `resolved_by` is `provisional` for a decision by that quorum, which the schema's check anticipated; the tiebreaker and the feed record their ruling here the same way, and the void rule expires it here. An edge between two account-holders is the debtor's to confirm (the yep, and the ordinary `confirm` path, which is Principle 2); an edge with a ghost on it waits for the bind, which moves it to the account. The settled screen shows the pending proposals beside any confirmed edges, and the story's consequences follow. `verify-envio` has nothing to check on such a market.
